<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/biosensors-15-00692-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>Before the experiment, the electrode cap was positioned on the participant’s head according to the 10–20 international placement system. Electrode gel was applied to each electrode location using a syringe to ensure optimal conductivity. The cap’s electrodes were then connected to the input pins of the Cyton and Daisy boards, which were wirelessly linked to the recording computer via the OpenBCI USB dongle. <a href="#biosensors-15-00692-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a> illustrates the setup procedure.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="biosensors-15-00692-f001"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12564669_biosensors-15-00692-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/465e/12564669/17ddbbb2efeb/biosensors-15-00692-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="615" width="649" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/biosensors-15-00692-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Motor imagery EEG experiment setup showing a participant with an OpenBCI EEG cap interacting with the Unity-based stimulus interface.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The experimental paradigm was implemented using a custom interface developed in Unity (<a href="#biosensors-15-00692-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>). After the participant’s information was entered and a specific hand movement class was selected (<a href="#biosensors-15-00692-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). The particular hand movement classes were inspired by the work of Ofner et al. [<a href="#B28-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-biosensors-15-00692">28</a>], who demonstrated their effectiveness in studies involving patients with spinal cord injury (SCI). The experiment began with a predefined sequence of visual stimuli. The protocol consisted of five repetitions per class, with a 3 s start delay, a 5 s stimulus duration (fixation cross), followed by a 3 s rest period. Each trial concluded with a 3 s post-stimulus delay.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="biosensors-15-00692-f002"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12564669_biosensors-15-00692-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/465e/12564669/87fb285e7a11/biosensors-15-00692-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="599" width="774" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/biosensors-15-00692-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Visual components of the EEG experimental protocol. The parameter configuration interface is used before each session.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="biosensors-15-00692-f003"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12564669_biosensors-15-00692-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/465e/12564669/f86d4e093614/biosensors-15-00692-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="170" width="758" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/biosensors-15-00692-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Visual stimuli were shown to participants to prompt imagined motor execution [<a href="#B28-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-biosensors-15-00692">28</a>].</p></figcaption></figure><p>During each trial, participants were instructed to perform motor imagery of the displayed hand movement (e.g., left hand open, right hand grasp) while minimizing physical motion. The experimental flow involved an initial blank screen, followed by the appearance of a fixation cross, a rest interval, and finally the visual cue corresponding to the intended movement (<a href="#biosensors-15-00692-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>). Upon completion of all repetitions, a message indicating the end of the experiment was displayed.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="biosensors-15-00692-f004"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12564669_biosensors-15-00692-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/465e/12564669/16e495ff04d7/biosensors-15-00692-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="421" width="688" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/biosensors-15-00692-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Flow diagram of the EEG experimental protocol. Each trial begins with a start delay followed by a fixation cross display to direct participant focus. A rest period is then introduced before the motor imagery stimulus (i.e., hand movement) is shown. After the participant imagines the movement, another rest period is initiated. Finally, an end delay marks the conclusion of the iteration with an exit message.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The EEG recording process was initiated simultaneously with the start of the experiment via the OpenBCI GUI, allowing synchronized signal acquisition and stimulus presentation. The paradigm is aligned with standard motor imagery-based BCI protocols, where subjects are encouraged to mentally rehearse the movement in the absence of actual execution, which has been shown to activate relevant cortical motor areas [<a href="#B29-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-biosensors-15-00692">29</a>,<a href="#B30-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-biosensors-15-00692">30</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. EEG Dataset Description and Preprocessing</h3>
<p>EEG signals were acquired from six healthy volunteers performing five motor imagery tasks. EEG signals were acquired from six healthy volunteers performing five motor imagery tasks. The recordings were collected using a 32-channel EEG acquisition system (BrainVision Recorder, Brain Products GmbH, Gilching, Germany, v1.22) at a sampling rate of 512 Hz. Data preprocessing and feature extraction were conducted using MATLAB R2024a (MathWorks, Natick, MA, USA) [<a href="#B31-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-biosensors-15-00692">31</a>], and machine learning pipelines were implemented on Orange Data Mining v3.36 [<a href="#B32-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-biosensors-15-00692">32</a>]. All software versions and references are provided to ensure reproducibility.</p>
<p>The raw EEG signals acquired through the OpenBCI GUI platform were initially subjected to preprocessing operations within the same platform. A band-pass filter was applied to retain frequencies within a specified range, allowing only signals between 5.0 Hz and 50.0 Hz to pass, shown in <a href="#biosensors-15-00692-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>. This frequency window effectively preserves relevant neural oscillations typically associated with motor tasks, including the mu (8–13 Hz), beta (13–30 Hz), and low gamma (30–50 Hz) bands, while attenuating lower-frequency drifts and high-frequency noise.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="biosensors-15-00692-f005"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12564669_biosensors-15-00692-g005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/465e/12564669/bffa836ecc1c/biosensors-15-00692-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="376" width="756" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/biosensors-15-00692-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Example EEG signal from a representative channel before (<strong>top</strong>) and after (<strong>bottom</strong>) preprocessing. A 5–50 Hz band-pass filter and a 50 Hz notch filter were applied to preserve motor-related rhythms (mu: 8–13 Hz, beta: 13–30 Hz, low gamma: 30–50 Hz) and remove power-line noise.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Subsequently, a notch filter was employed to suppress power line interference typically observed around 50 Hz to 60 Hz, thereby improving signal fidelity. A fourth-order Butterworth filter was selected as the filter type due to its advantageous characteristics, including minimal phase distortion, smooth frequency response, and flat passband behavior. The choice of a fourth-order filter ensures adequate sharpness in the transition band while maintaining the integrity of the EEG signal structure, which is crucial for subsequent feature extraction and classification tasks [<a href="#B7-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-biosensors-15-00692">7</a>,<a href="#B28-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-biosensors-15-00692">28</a>].</p>
<p>The active task intervals were identified as the 9–13th, 25–29th, 41–45th, 57–61st, and 73–77th seconds of each trial. Each signal was then normalized using z-score normalization, a widely adopted technique in EEG signal preprocessing [<a href="#B8-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-biosensors-15-00692">8</a>]. The formula is defined as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD1-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm1" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Z</mi><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>X</mi><mo>−</mo><mi>μ</mi></mrow><mi>σ</mi></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(1)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <em>X</em> denotes an individual EEG data point, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm2" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>μ</mi></mrow></math></span> represents the mean of the EEG channel, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm3" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow></math></span> is the standard deviation of the same channel.</p>
<p>After normalization, all combinations of tasks related to motor imagery in grades 2, 3, and 5 were constructed, and the results were evaluated using a machine learning pipeline (<a href="#biosensors-15-00692-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>) implemented in the Orange Data Mining platform [<a href="#B32-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-biosensors-15-00692">32</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="biosensors-15-00692-f006"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12564669_biosensors-15-00692-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/465e/12564669/da12cb8c32de/biosensors-15-00692-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="536" width="728" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/biosensors-15-00692-f006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Workflow of the classification pipeline implemented in Orange Data Mining software. Raw EEG datasets are imported and preprocessed through individual Data Table modules, then concatenated into a unified dataset. Manifold learning methods are applied for dimensionality reduction, followed by classification using k-nearest neighbors (<em>k</em>-NN), Support Vector Machine (SVM), and Naive Bayes classifiers. The final evaluation is performed using the Test and Score module.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot3-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Manifold Learning Methods</h3>
<section id="sec2dot3dot1-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.1. Multi-Dimensional Scaling (MDS)</h4>
<p>Multi-Dimensional Scaling (MDS) is a non-linear, unsupervised dimensionality reduction technique that represents the pairwise dissimilarities between high-dimensional data points in a lower-dimensional space while preserving their relative distances [<a href="#B33-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-biosensors-15-00692">33</a>]. MDS is commonly employed in exploratory and multivariate data analysis and has gained considerable attention in recent years [<a href="#B34-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-biosensors-15-00692">34</a>]. There are three main variants of MDS-classical, metric, and non-metric each suited to different types of dissimilarity data and analysis objectives [<a href="#B35-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-biosensors-15-00692">35</a>].</p>
<p>In classical MDS, the pairwise distances between samples in the input data matrix <em>X</em> are computed using the Euclidean distance metric, which is most appropriate for quantitative data. The Euclidean distance between two data points <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm4" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm5" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> in a high-dimensional space is calculated as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD2-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm6" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(2)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The classical MDS algorithm consists of two primary matrices: the squared distance matrix <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm7" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></math></span> and the centering matrix <em>H</em>. The matrix <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm8" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></math></span> contains squared Euclidean distances, while <em>H</em> is defined as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD3-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm9" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi><mo>−</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>n</mi></mfrac></mstyle><msup><mn>11</mn><mo>⊤</mo></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(3)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <em>I</em> is the identity matrix of size <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm10" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>×</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span>, and 1 is a column vector of ones of length <em>n</em>.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD4-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm11" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>B</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle><mi>H</mi><msup><mi>D</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mi>H</mi></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(4)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The final step involves the eigenvalue decomposition of the matrix <em>B</em>, yielding a set of eigenvalues and eigenvectors. The coordinates in the reduced-dimensional space are then obtained by</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD5-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm12" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Y</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>U</mi><msup><mo>Λ</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(5)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <em>U</em> is the matrix of eigenvectors and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm13" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mo>Λ</mo></mrow></math></span> is the diagonal matrix of eigenvalues. This transformation allows the high-dimensional data to be embedded into a low-dimensional space that preserves the original pairwise distances as closely as possible [<a href="#B36-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-biosensors-15-00692">36</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot2-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.2. Isometric Feature Mapping (ISOMAP)</h4>
<p>Isometric Feature Mapping (ISOMAP) is a non-linear dimensionality reduction technique that extends classical Multi-Dimensional Scaling (MDS) by incorporating geodesic distances rather than Euclidean distances. In contrast to linear methods, ISOMAP is designed to reveal the intrinsic geometry of high-dimensional data lying on a non-linear manifold by approximating the true geodesic distances between all pairs of points [<a href="#B11-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-biosensors-15-00692">11</a>,<a href="#B37-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-biosensors-15-00692">37</a>,<a href="#B38-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-biosensors-15-00692">38</a>].</p>
<p>The algorithm operates through three main steps:</p>
<p>Step 1: Construction of the Neighborhood Graph</p>
<p>A neighborhood graph is constructed to represent local relationships among data points. This can be achieved using either the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm14" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow></math></span>-neighborhood criterion, where each point is connected to all others within a fixed radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm15" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow></math></span>, or the <em>k</em>-nearest neighbors (<em>k</em>-NN) approach, where each point is connected to its <em>k</em> closest neighbors. The resulting graph is weighted, with edges reflecting Euclidean distances between neighboring points, denoted as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm16" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>x</mi></msub><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></span>. This graph approximates the local structure of the underlying manifold [<a href="#B11-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-biosensors-15-00692">11</a>].</p>
<p>Step 2: Estimation of Geodesic Distances</p>
<p>In manifold learning, the shortest path between two points is measured along the curved surface of the manifold, referred to as the geodesic distance. ISOMAP approximates these distances by computing the shortest path through the neighborhood graph using algorithms such as Dijkstra’s or Floyd-Warshall. This results in a geodesic distance matrix <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm17" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>G</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, which approximates the pairwise intrinsic distances between all data points [<a href="#B39-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-biosensors-15-00692">39</a>].</p>
<p>Step 3: Application of Classical MDS</p>
<p>Once the geodesic distance matrix <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm18" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>G</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is computed, classical MDS is applied to this matrix to generate a low-dimensional embedding. The goal is to find a mapping <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm19" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>Y</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>m</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span> that best preserves the pairwise geodesic distances in the reduced space. This step involves double-centering of the matrix and eigenvalue decomposition, similar to classical MDS, but using <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm20" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>G</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> instead of Euclidean distances [<a href="#B38-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-biosensors-15-00692">38</a>,<a href="#B40-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-biosensors-15-00692">40</a>].</p>
<p>ISOMAP thus enables the unfolding of complex manifolds by preserving global geometric structures and providing a meaningful low-dimensional representation suitable for classification, visualization, or further analysis.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot3-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.3. Local Linear Embedding (LLE)</h4>
<p>The Locally Linear Embedding (LLE) is recognized as an unsupervised learning algorithm through which a low-dimensional embedding of high-dimensional inputs is computed, with neighborhood relationships being preserved [<a href="#B41-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-biosensors-15-00692">41</a>].</p>
<p>In contrast to clustering techniques that are employed to reduce the dimensionality of local data, LLE effectively transforms its inputs into a singular global coordinate system that possesses a lower dimensionality. Furthermore, the optimization processes of LLE are not subject to local minimum. LLE’s employment of local symmetries inherent in linear reconstructions facilitates the acquisition of knowledge concerning the overall structure of nonlinear manifolds. This capability is particularly pronounced in scenarios where these manifolds are derived from facial images or textual documents [<a href="#B10-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-biosensors-15-00692">10</a>].</p>
<p>LLE endeavors to preserve the local affine structure. Under the assumption that each data point lies within a manifold’s locally linear patch, LLE characterizes the local geometry by representing each data point as an approximate affine combination of its neighboring points. After this process, LLE accomplishes a reduction in dimension through the construction of a scatter of points in low dimensions, ensuring the optimal maintenance of the affine combination coefficients obtained from the high-dimensional data space [<a href="#B42-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-biosensors-15-00692">42</a>].</p>
<p>It is required to find the <em>k</em>-NN for each data point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm21" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> in the data set. This particular process is founded on the assumption that a small linear segment on a larger manifold is formed collectively by these data points. In the context of these local segments, it is possible to establish linear coefficients that facilitate the reconstruction of each observation based on its neighboring observations. The accuracy of the reconstructed data can be determined by calculating the total squared dissimilarity between each data point and the reconstruction based on that point. In addition, the weight matrix <em>W</em> can be determined by minimizing the sequence error.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD6-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm22" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>E</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>W</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msup><mfenced separators="" open="|" close="|"><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mfenced><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(6)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The process involves the computation of the squared distances between all data points and their respective reconstructions. The contribution of each data point to the reconstruction of the corresponding point is represented by the assigned weights. The calculation of the weights <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm23" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> is done by minimizing the cost function, subject to two restrictions. The first constraint specifies that each data point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm24" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is reconstructed exclusively from its neighbors, thereby forcing</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD7-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm25" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(7)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
if <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm26" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> does not belong to this set. The index <em>j</em> in the equation indicates the data points that are located within the k-nearest neighbors of the data point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm27" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, where the optimal weights in the error function are obtained using the least squares method under the constraint that the sum of the rows of the weight matrix equals one:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD8-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm28" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(8)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In the final phase of the algorithm, each high-dimensional observation, denoted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm29" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, is mapped to a low-dimensional vector denoted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm30" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> which denotes global internal coordinates on the manifold. This process is achieved by selecting d-dimensional coordinates, denoted as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm31" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, to minimize the embedding cost function, thereby ensuring the optimal representation of the data.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD9-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm32" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo>Φ</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>Y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msup><mfenced separators="" open="|" close="|"><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mfenced><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The sparse eigenvalue-eigenvector approach is a viable method to solve the minimization problem. The procedure for obtaining a symmetric and semi-positive N<em>x</em>.</p>
<p>N-dimensional sparse matrix for eigenvalue decomposition is described in equation form.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD10-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm33" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>I</mi><mo>−</mo><mi>W</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>I</mi><mo>−</mo><mi>W</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mi>T</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
Independent coordinates centered at the origin are provided by the eigenvectors associated with the smallest non-zero eigenvalue <em>M</em> of the <em>d</em>-matrix.</p>
<p>The LLE can be summarizen as follows: (1) The determination of <em>k</em> for the neighborhood, as well as the number of dimensions <em>d</em> in the reduced coordinate system, is required. Then the neighbors of each data point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm34" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> are calculated based on the selected <em>k</em>. (2) The weights <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm35" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, which represent the contribution of each data point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm36" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> to its nearest neighbors, are calculated by reconstructing <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm37" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> as a linear combination of its neighbors. These weights are calculated by minimizing the cost function through constrained linear optimization to ensure the most accurate representation of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm38" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> in its local neighborhood. (3) The points <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm39" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> are constructed in reduced d-dimensional space to ensure that the weights <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm40" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> remain consistent. This is achieved by computing the vectors <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm41" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> that can best be reconstructed by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm42" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, minimizing the quadratic form in the equation using the smallest non-zero eigenvectors [<a href="#B10-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-biosensors-15-00692">10</a>,<a href="#B36-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-biosensors-15-00692">36</a>,<a href="#B43-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-biosensors-15-00692">43</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot4-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.4. t-Distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding (t-SNE)</h4>
<p>t-Distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding (t-SNE) is a nonlinear, unsupervised dimensionality reduction technique introduced by Laurens van der Maaten and Geoffrey Hinton in 2008. It has been widely employed for the visualization and exploration of high-dimensional data, particularly due to its ability to preserve local neighborhood structures when projecting data into a lower-dimensional space.</p>
<p>t-SNE minimizes the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between two probability distributions: one that measures pairwise similarities of data points in the high-dimensional space and another that measures pairwise similarities in the low-dimensional embedding. This cost function is non-convex, and therefore, different initializations may lead to different solutions. Nonetheless, t-SNE is particularly effective at revealing clusters and complex data structures at multiple scales [<a href="#B44-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-biosensors-15-00692">44</a>].</p>
<p>The algorithm operates in three main steps:</p>
<section id="sec12"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 1: Compute High-Dimensional Similarities</h5>
<p>A Gaussian distribution centered at each data point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm43" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is used to model pairwise similarities in the high-dimensional space. The conditional probability <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm44" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>|</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> indicates the likelihood that point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm45" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> would be a neighbor of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm46" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD11-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm47" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>|</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mo form="prefix">exp</mo><mfenced separators="" open="(" close=")"><mo>−</mo><mfrac><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mrow><mn>2</mn><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow></mfrac></mfenced></mrow><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>≠</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mo form="prefix">exp</mo><mfenced separators="" open="(" close=")"><mo>−</mo><mfrac><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mrow><mn>2</mn><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow></mfrac></mfenced></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(11)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>To obtain a symmetric joint probability, the following is computed:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD12-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm48" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>|</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>|</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(12)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <em>N</em> is the total number of data points.</p></section><section id="sec13"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 2: Compute Low-Dimensional Similarities</h5>
<p>In the low-dimensional space, the similarity between points <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm49" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm50" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is modeled using a heavy-tailed Student’s t-distribution with one degree of freedom:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD13-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm51" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>q</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mrow><msubsup><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>≠</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow><mi>n</mi></msubsup><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(13)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="sec14"><h5 class="pmc_sec_title">Step 3: Minimize the Cost Function</h5>
<p>The divergence between high- and low-dimensional similarity distributions is quantified using the KL divergence:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD14-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm52" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>i</mi><mo>≠</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo form="prefix">log</mo><mfenced separators="" open="(" close=")"><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mi>q</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mfrac></mstyle></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(14)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>This cost is minimized using gradient descent, with updates given by</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD15-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm53" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mo>∂</mo><mi>C</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>∂</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>≠</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>p</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>q</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(15)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
Summary</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>High-dimensional similarities are modeled using Gaussian distributions.</p></li>
<li><p>Low-dimensional similarities are captured using a Student’s t-distribution.</p></li>
<li><p>The KL divergence is minimized to produce a faithful low-dimensional embedding that retains local structure.</p></li>
</ul></section></section></section><section id="sec2dot4-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Spectral Embedding</h3>
<p>Spectral embedding is a nonlinear dimensionality reduction technique that leverages the principles of spectral graph theory. It uses the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of matrices derived from data similarity graphs to project high-dimensional data into a lower-dimensional space while preserving essential structural relationships [<a href="#B45-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-biosensors-15-00692">45</a>,<a href="#B46-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-biosensors-15-00692">46</a>].</p>
<p>This technique assumes that the data lie on a low-dimensional manifold embedded within a high-dimensional space. Spectral embedding algorithms, therefore, aim to reveal the intrinsic geometry of this manifold by constructing a graph of data points and analyzing its spectral properties.</p>
<p>In data classification problems, each temporal segment is treated as an individual data instance. Let <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm54" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>m</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span> denote the vector of Fourier coefficients associated with the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm55" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mi>h</mi></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span> time segment, where <em>m</em> is the total number of coefficients. Since many coefficients may carry redundant or insignificant information, cosine distance is chosen as the dissimilarity metric due to its robustness against small-magnitude variations:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD16-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm56" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>·</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>∥</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mrow><mo>∥</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>∥</mo></mrow></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(16)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Here, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm57" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> denotes the cosine distance between points <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm58" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm59" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, and the distance matrix <em>D</em> is computed from all pairwise distances.</p>
<p>A Gaussian similarity function is applied to transform the distances into affinity scores:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD17-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm60" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo form="prefix">exp</mo><mfenced separators="" open="(" close=")"><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mo>−</mo><msubsup><mi>d</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mrow><msubsup><mi>σ</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></mfrac></mstyle></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(17)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm61" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> is defined as the distance to the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm62" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mi>N</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mi>h</mi></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span> nearest neighbor of point <em>i</em>, promoting adaptive local scaling.</p>
<p>To create a sparse affinity matrix <em>S</em>, only the top <em>N</em> similarities for each data point are preserved, and all other values are set to zero. A diagonal degree matrix <em>D</em> is then computed as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD18-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm63" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><msub><mi>S</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(18)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Using these matrices, the normalized graph Laplacian is constructed:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD19-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm64" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>I</mi><mo>−</mo><msup><mi>D</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></msup><mi>S</mi><msup><mi>D</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(19)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The eigenvectors corresponding to the smallest non-zero eigenvalues of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm65" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> form the low-dimensional embedding of the data. This procedure enables the mapping of complex, high-dimensional structures into a simplified representation while maintaining locality and manifold topology [<a href="#B47-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-biosensors-15-00692">47</a>].</p></section></section><section id="sec3-biosensors-15-00692"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Classifiers</h2>
<section id="sec3dot1-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Support Vector Machine (SVM)</h3>
<p>Support Vector Machine (SVM) is a supervised learning algorithm introduced by Vapnik [<a href="#B48-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-biosensors-15-00692">48</a>], and it has demonstrated strong performance in various real-world classification problems, including brain-computer interface (BCI) applications [<a href="#B16-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-biosensors-15-00692">16</a>,<a href="#B36-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-biosensors-15-00692">36</a>]. SVM aims to find an optimal hyperplane that maximally separates data points of different classes in a high-dimensional space, thereby minimizing generalization error.</p>
<section id="sec3dot1dot1-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1.1. Step 1: Fundamental Concepts of SVM</h4>
<p>SVM identifies a hyperplane <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm66" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> to separate two classes (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm67" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm68" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>)</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD20-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm69" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mi>T</mi></msup><mi>x</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>b</mi></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(20)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm70" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the weight vector normal to the hyperplane,</p></li>
<li><p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm71" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>b</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the bias,</p></li>
<li><p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm72" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the input data point.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>To ensure correct classification, the condition below must hold:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD21-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm73" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mfenced separators="" open="(" close=")"><msup><mi>w</mi><mi>T</mi></msup><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>b</mi></mfenced><mo>≥</mo><mn>1</mn><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mo>∀</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(21)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="sec3dot1dot2-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1.2. Step 2: Optimization Problem</h4>
<p>The margin is maximized by minimizing <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm74" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><mi>w</mi><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></math></span>, leading to the following convex optimization problem:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD22-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm75" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo movablelimits="true" form="prefix">min</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><mi>w</mi><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>subject</mi><mspace width="4.pt"></mspace><mi>to</mi><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mfenced separators="" open="(" close=")"><msup><mi>w</mi><mi>T</mi></msup><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>b</mi></mfenced><mo>≥</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(22)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="sec3dot1dot3-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1.3. Step 3: Lagrange Multipliers and Dual Form</h4>
<p>Using Lagrange multipliers <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm76" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, the dual problem becomes</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD23-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm77" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><munder><mo movablelimits="true" form="prefix">max</mo><mi>α</mi></munder><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>T</mi></msubsup><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(23)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD24-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm78" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>subject</mi><mspace width="4.pt"></mspace><mi>to</mi><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(24)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The optimal weight vector is</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD25-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm79" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msub><mi>α</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>and</mi><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>b</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mi>T</mi></msup><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(25)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="sec3dot1dot4-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1.4. Step 4: Kernel and Soft Margin</h4>
<p>This study employs the Radial Basis Function (RBF) kernel:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD26-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm80" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>K</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mo form="prefix">exp</mo><mfenced separators="" open="(" close=")"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mi>γ</mi><mo>∥</mo></mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(26)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>To handle overlapping classes, slack variables <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm81" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>ξ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> are introduced:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD27-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm82" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo movablelimits="true" form="prefix">min</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mstyle><msup><mrow><mo>∥</mo><mi>w</mi><mo>∥</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mi>C</mi><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><msub><mi>ξ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>subject</mi><mspace width="4.pt"></mspace><mi>to</mi><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mrow><mo>(</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mi>T</mi></msup><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>b</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>ξ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><msub><mi>ξ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(27)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Here, <em>C</em> is the regularization parameter balancing margin maximization and classification error [<a href="#B49-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-biosensors-15-00692">49</a>].</p></section></section><section id="sec3dot2-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. <em>k</em>-Nearest Neighbors (<em>k</em>-NN)</h3>
<p>The <em>k</em>-nearest neighbor (<em>k</em>-NN) is a non-parametric, supervised learning algorithm that classifies data points based on the majority vote of their <em>k</em> closest neighbors [<a href="#B50-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-biosensors-15-00692">50</a>]. It is widely used due to its simplicity and effectiveness, particularly in multi-class problems.</p>
<p>The core idea is to assign a class to a new observation by evaluating the distance to <em>k</em> training samples. The most common metric is Euclidean distance, defined as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD28-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm83" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>d</mi></munderover><msup><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(28)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The choice of <em>k</em> significantly influences performance. A small <em>k</em> may be sensitive to noise, while a large <em>k</em> may blur class boundaries. Thus, an odd <em>k</em> is often used to avoid ties in binary classification [<a href="#B51-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-biosensors-15-00692">51</a>,<a href="#B52-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-biosensors-15-00692">52</a>].</p></section><section id="sec3dot3-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Naive Bayes</h3>
<p>Naive Bayes is a probabilistic classifier grounded in Bayes’ theorem. It assumes strong (Naive) independence between features given the class label. Despite this often unrealistic assumption, Naive Bayes classifiers have demonstrated competitive performance in various practical applications due to their simplicity, efficiency, and robustness.</p>
<section id="sec3dot3dot1-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3.1. Step 1: Bayes’ Theorem</h4>
<p>The core of the Naive Bayes classifier is Bayes’ theorem, which expresses the posterior probability <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm84" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>∣</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> of a class <em>y</em> given a feature vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm85" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD29-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm86" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>∣</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>∣</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(29)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm87" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>∣</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the posterior probability of class <em>y</em> given features <em>x</em>,</p></li>
<li><p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm88" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the prior probability of class <em>y</em>,</p></li>
<li><p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm89" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>∣</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the likelihood of features <em>x</em> given class <em>y</em>,</p></li>
<li><p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm90" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the evidence or marginal likelihood of observing <em>x</em> (often omitted in classification as it is constant across classes).</p></li>
</ul></section><section id="sec3dot3dot2-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3.2. Step 2: Conditional Independence Assumption</h4>
<p>The fundamental assumption of Naive Bayes is that the features <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm91" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> are conditionally independent given the class label <em>y</em>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD30-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm92" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>∣</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>∣</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(30)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>This simplifies the computation of the posterior as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD31-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm93" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>∣</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mo>∝</mo><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><munderover><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>∣</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(31)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="sec3dot3dot3-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3.3. Step 3: Classification Decision</h4>
<p>The final prediction is made by selecting the class <em>y</em> that maximizes the posterior probability:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD32-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm94" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">^</mo></mover><mo>=</mo><mo form="prefix">arg</mo><munder><mo movablelimits="true" form="prefix">max</mo><mi>y</mi></munder><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><munderover><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><mi>P</mi><mrow><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>∣</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(32)</td>
</tr></table></section></section><section id="sec3dot4-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. Hyperparameter Tuning and Cross-Validation</h3>
<p>To ensure reproducibility and fair comparison, all hyperparameters for manifold learning and classification algorithms were optimized using a systematic grid search approach. For t-SNE, perplexity values were tested in the range of 5–50, with a step size of 5, and learning rates between 100 and 1000. For ISOMAP and LLE, the number of neighbors was varied between 5 and 20. The SVM classifier was tuned over <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm95" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>{</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>100</mn><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm96" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>γ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>{</mo><mn>0.001</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.01</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. For <em>k</em>-NN, <em>k</em> values from 1 to 15 were tested. Nested cross-validation (inner loop for hyperparameter optimization, outer loop for performance estimation) was employed to avoid overfitting. The final selected parameters were those maximizing mean accuracy on the validation sets.</p>
<p>Dimensionality reduction and classification steps were executed with predefined hyperparameters, as outlined below.</p>
<p>Manifold Learning Algorithms:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>Multi-Dimensional Scaling (MDS): Maximum iterations set to 300, initialized using PCA.</p></li>
<li><p>Isometric Mapping (ISOMAP): Neighborhood size of 5 [<a href="#B11-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-biosensors-15-00692">11</a>].</p></li>
<li><p>Local Linear Embedding (LLE): Standard LLE method with 10 neighbors and a maximum of 100 iterations [<a href="#B10-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-biosensors-15-00692">10</a>].</p></li>
<li><p>t-Distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding (t-SNE): Parameters include Euclidean metric, perplexity of 30, early exaggeration of 8, learning rate of 20, maximum of 1000 iterations, and PCA initialization [<a href="#B13-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-biosensors-15-00692">13</a>].</p></li>
<li><p>Spectral Embedding: Affinity set to “nearest neighbors” as recommended in [<a href="#B14-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-biosensors-15-00692">14</a>].</p></li>
</ul>
<p>All manifold learning algorithms were configured to reduce the original EEG signal features into a three-dimensional subspace, facilitating visualization and efficient classification.</p>
<p>Classification Algorithms:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>k-Nearest Neighbors (<em>k</em>-NN): Configured with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm97" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>, using the Euclidean distance metric and uniform weighting.</p></li>
<li><p>Support Vector Machine (SVM): Configured with a cost parameter <em>C</em> = 1.00, epsilon = 0.10, and radial basis function (RBF) kernel; numerical tolerance was set to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm98" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mn>0.001</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>, with a maximum of 100 iterations.</p></li>
<li><p>Naïve Bayes: Implemented based on Bayes’ Theorem, assuming conditional independence among features.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This comprehensive pipeline enabled the systematic evaluation of different manifold learning and classification methods for EEG-based motor intention decoding tasks in healthy individuals.</p></section><section id="sec3dot5-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Evaluation of Manifold Learning Algorithms Performance</h3>
<p>In this study, the performance evaluation of manifold learning algorithms was conducted using stratified <em>k</em>-fold cross-validation with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm99" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>. In this procedure, the dataset is partitioned into five equal subsets while preserving the class distribution. During each iteration, one subset is reserved as the test set, and the remaining four subsets are used for training. This process is repeated five times, and the final performance is computed as the average of the individual results. The use of stratified sampling ensures that the class balance is maintained in each fold, thereby yielding a more realistic and robust estimation of model performance.</p>
<p>To assess the efficacy of the manifold learning algorithms, standard evaluation metrics were employed, including Area Under the Curve (AUC), classification accuracy (CA), F1-score, and precision. These metrics offer complementary insights into the classification model’s effectiveness, especially in the context of binary classification problems.</p>
<p>In binary classification, each instance is assigned to one of two classes: positive or negative. The outcomes of a classifier can be summarized in a confusion matrix, which categorizes predictions as follows:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>True Positives (TPs): Correctly predicted positive instances.</p></li>
<li><p>False Positives (FPs): Incorrectly predicted as positive when they are negative.</p></li>
<li><p>True Negatives (TNs): Correctly predicted negative instances.</p></li>
<li><p>False Negatives (FNs): Incorrectly predicted as negative when they are positive.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This framework facilitates a comprehensive understanding of the model’s predictive capabilities [<a href="#B53-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-biosensors-15-00692">53</a>,<a href="#B54-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-biosensors-15-00692">54</a>].</p>
<section id="sec3dot5dot1-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.1. Area Under the Curve (AUC)</h4>
<p>The Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) Curve, abbreviated as AUC, is a widely used metric that quantifies the classifier’s ability to distinguish between classes. The ROC curve plots the True Positive Rate (TPR) against the False Positive Rate (FPR) at various threshold settings. A higher AUC value indicates better overall classification performance.</p>
<p>The TPR (or sensitivity) and the True Negative Rate (TNR or specificity) are calculated as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD33-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm100" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1.em"></mspace><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(33)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>AUC provides an aggregate measure of performance across all classification thresholds, making it particularly valuable in imbalanced datasets [<a href="#B55-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-biosensors-15-00692">55</a>,<a href="#B56-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-biosensors-15-00692">56</a>].</p></section><section id="sec3dot5dot2-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.2. Classification Accuracy (CA)</h4>
<p>Classification accuracy (CA) measures the proportion of correctly predicted instances (both positive and negative) over the total number of samples:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD34-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm101" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>C</mi><mi>A</mi><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(34)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Although widely used, accuracy alone may be misleading in imbalanced datasets. Therefore, additional metrics such as F1-score and precision are necessary for a more nuanced evaluation [<a href="#B4-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-biosensors-15-00692">4</a>,<a href="#B57-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-biosensors-15-00692">57</a>].</p></section><section id="sec3dot5dot3-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.3. F1 Score</h4>
<p>The F1-score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall and is especially useful when dealing with class imbalance. It is defined as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD35-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm102" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>·</mo><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>·</mo><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(35)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>A higher F1-score indicates a better trade-off between precision and recall, especially when false positives and false negatives carry different costs [<a href="#B58-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-biosensors-15-00692">58</a>,<a href="#B59-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-biosensors-15-00692">59</a>].</p></section><section id="sec3dot5dot4-biosensors-15-00692"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.4. Precision</h4>
<p>Precision is defined as the proportion of true positives among all instances classified as positive:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD36-biosensors-15-00692"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm103" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mstyle scriptlevel="0" displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow></mfrac></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(36)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>It evaluates the classifier’s exactness and is crucial when the cost of false positives is high [<a href="#B53-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-biosensors-15-00692">53</a>].</p></section></section><section id="sec3dot6-biosensors-15-00692"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Data Partitioning and Leakage Prevention</h3>
<p>To prevent data leakage, raw EEG trials were first segmented and assigned at the subject level before any feature extraction or dimensionality reduction steps. All preprocessing, including filtering, dimensionality reduction (ISOMAP, LLE, Spectral Embedding, t-SNE, MDS), and classifier training was performed strictly within training folds.</p>
<p>The Orange Data Mining software’s Pipeline Builder was used only to construct modular workflows; training and validation data were kept strictly separated, and nested 5 × 5-fold cross-validation was applied. This ensured no information from validation/test folds leaked into the training pipeline.</p></section></section><section id="sec4-biosensors-15-00692"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Results</h2>
<p>This section reports the performance of each manifold learning method (ISOMAP, LLE, Spectral Embedding, t-SNE, MDS) combined with three classifiers (SVM, <em>k</em>-NN, Naïve Bayes) across two-, three-, and five-class settings. We summarize metrics as AUC, CA, F1, and precision without interpretation; detailed comparisons and implications are discussed in <a href="#sec5-biosensors-15-00692" class="usa-link">Section 5</a>.</p>
<p>According to the results shown in <a href="#biosensors-15-00692-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, the most effective classification method in the ISOMAP method is <em>k</em>-NN. The values of AUC, CA, F1 score, and precision are between 99.5% and 99.3%, indicating that the combination of ISOMAP and <em>k</em>-NN is quite successful. As the number of classes increases, the performances of all classifiers decrease, but this is expected. Naive Bayes shows the best performance after <em>k</em>-NN. However, a serious decrease is seen in the five-class case. SVM is the model with the lowest performance in all classes.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/biosensors-15-00692-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p><a href="#biosensors-15-00692-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> compares the performance of the classification algorithms (SVM, <em>k</em>-NN, Naive Bayes) applied after dimensionality reduction with the Local Linear Embedding (LLE) method in two-, three-, and five-class classifications through AUC, CA, F1, and precision metrics. <em>k</em>-NN is the model with the highest metric value in all classes (two, three, five). According to the metrics, <em>k</em>-NN is between 96.3–81.5% SVM is the model with the lowest performance. According to the SVM results, some values remained around or below 0.5. This shows that the model has weak discrimination power between classes. The Naive Bayes method is the method with the best results after <em>k</em>-NN.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/biosensors-15-00692-t003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p><a href="#biosensors-15-00692-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a> shows the performances of the classification algorithms (SVM, <em>k</em>-NN, Naive Bayes) with the Spectral Embedding method in two-, three- and five-class classifications. <em>k</em>-NN showed the highest performance in all metrics, staying between 96–72.1%. Although SVM gave a very high AUC (96%) value, especially in the three-class case, it showed low performance in other metrics. Naive Bayes gave worse results than <em>k</em>-NN but better than SVM. Although SVM reached the highest value in AUC with 96% in three-class values, it failed in other metrics (CA = 39%, F1 = 36.5%). This shows that the model cannot provide balance while trying to distinguish the classes.</p>
